# Splitting datum into server mode + thin-client mode — research report

## 1. Codebase findings: every localhost/oMLX assumption

All paths are in `/Volumes/Extra/repos/gitrdunhq/datum` unless noted.

### `datum/local_llm.py` — the choke point (good news: it's the only one)

| Location | Assumption | Why it breaks a thin client |
|---|---|---|
| `local_llm.py:103-113` `is_available()` | Hard-gates on `platform.system()=="Darwin" and machine=="arm64"` **plus** `import mlx_lm` | **The #1 blocker.** `should_use_local()` (`:1463-1469`) calls this first, so a client on Linux/Intel or without `mlx_lm` installed can *never* use a remote endpoint, even with a reachable `omlx_url`. Availability must become "endpoint reachable OR local MLX importable". |
| `local_llm.py:575-595` `chat()` | Always calls `load_model()` to run `tokenizer.apply_chat_template` — *before* `generate()` re-routes to oMLX | Requires the 19GB model load (or at least mlx_lm + HF tokenizer) on the client even when the server does the templating. Remote path should send `messages` straight to `/v1/chat/completions`. |
| `local_llm.py:145-169` `count_tokens()` / `check_context_budget()` | Local tokenizer via `load_model()` | Used by `generate()` fallback and `multi_turn_phase()` (`:1020`). Thin client needs an estimate (chars/4) or server-side `usage.prompt_tokens` feedback instead. |
| `local_llm.py:992-999, 1041-1048` | Builds an `mlx_lm` `make_prompt_cache` and slices prompts by cached token offset | Pure local-path optimization; meaningless against HTTP. Must be skipped on remote (it already silently `except: pass`es, but the offset-slicing logic at `:1041` would corrupt remote prompts if `_mt_cache` ever materialized — it can't today, but the coupling is fragile). |
| `local_llm.py:1487-1489` `_omlx_url()` | Single bare config key `omlx_url` | No `api_key`, no per-tier endpoint, no TLS options. |
| `local_llm.py:1492-1507` `_omlx_available()` | GET `{url}/health` then `/v1/models`, `timeout=1`, **no auth header**, called on **every** `generate()`/`structured()` call | (a) 401s once the server requires a key (oMLX makes the key *mandatory* on 0.0.0.0 binds — see §3); (b) a 1s health probe per request adds RTT on remote links; (c) on health failure it **silently falls back to loading the model locally** (`:218-224`) — on a thin client that's an ImportError, on a fat client it's a surprise 19GB load. |
| `local_llm.py:1532-1576` `_omlx_generate()` | `urllib.request`, `Content-Type` only — no `Authorization`, no retries, fixed `timeout=300`, `time_s: 0.0` hardcoded | No auth; metrics (`_log_metric` tokens/sec) are already broken on the oMLX path; no retry/backoff violates your own DPS-200. |
| `local_llm.py:1579-1623` `_omlx_structured()` | Same as above | Same. |
| `local_llm.py:22-50` `DEFAULTS` | `kv_bits/kv_group_size/max_kv_size` are mlx_lm-local knobs; `model` is an MLX-community HF ID | Fine, but these belong in a `[local]`/profile namespace, not the shared client schema. |
| `local_llm.py:124-139` `load_model()` | `hf_cache_dir` → `HF_HUB_CACHE` | Server-side concern only. |

### Consumers (all funnel through local_llm — clean seam)

- `datum/agent_loop.py:26-33` imports `generate`, `structured`, `get_model_for_phase`, `load_config` — never touches mlx directly. `:415-419` resolves `think_model`/`decide_model` from `[local_llm]` keys; `:373` `THINK_SAMPLING` is Qwen-2507-specific (the per-model profile proposal absorbs this).
- `datum/cli.py:383-467` `datum local-llm` gates on `is_available()` and prints **"requires Apple Silicon (macOS arm64)"** (`:419-426, :458-467`) — wrong message in a remote-endpoint world.
- `datum/memory_semantic.py:17,39-41` — `mlx.core` embeddings (`jinaai/jina-embeddings-v5...-mlx`). Genuinely local; stays in the heavy extra (or later moves to a server `/v1/embeddings` call — oMLX supports embedding models).
- `datum-local/datum_local/tdd_driver.py:138,192-193` — reads `model`/`decide_model` from `[local_llm]`; inherits whatever schema datum adopts.

### Config + packaging

- `.datum/config.toml:39` — `omlx_url = "http://localhost:12200"` (note: oMLX upstream default is **8000**; 12200 is your local override).
- `assets/config.toml.default:24-27` — there is already a **tier precedent**: `[models] reasoning/standard/fast` ("DATUM references tiers, never raw model IDs"). The local stack ignores it; the new schema should converge on it.
- `pyproject.toml:11-26` — **the packaging split half-exists.** Base deps are already thin (typer, rich, pydantic, pyyaml — no torch anywhere). All heavy deps (mlx, mlx-lm, huggingface_hub, tokenizers, numpy, outlines) live in the `memory` extra. The thin client is blocked by *code paths*, not dependencies.

## 2. Prior art (sources in linked docs)

**oMLX itself** ([github.com/jundot/omlx](https://github.com/jundot/omlx), Apache 2.0):
- Binds `127.0.0.1:8000` by default; admin toggle / flag for `0.0.0.0` ([#618](https://github.com/jundot/omlx/issues/618)). Config: `~/.omlx/settings.json`, `OMLX_PORT`, admin UI at `/admin`.
- **Native API-key auth**: `omlx serve --api-key …`; dmg app forces a key at first launch ([#564](https://github.com/jundot/omlx/issues/564)); clients send `Authorization: Bearer`. Key is **mandatory** when bound to 0.0.0.0. Single shared key only (multi-key is an open issue [#87](https://github.com/jundot/omlx/issues/87)).
- **Concurrency**: continuous batching (mlx-lm BatchGenerator), `--max-concurrent-requests` default **8** — two devs hitting the box decode in parallel within one batch, not queued serially.
- **Multi-model**: multiple models resident; LRU eviction under memory pressure; per-model pinning + idle TTL; memory guard defaults to RAM−8GB. 19GB Qwen + small Gemma coexist comfortably on 64GB.
- **Prefix cache**: vLLM-style PagedCacheManager, content-keyed **per model, global across clients**, hot RAM tier + SSD tier (`--paged-ssd-cache-dir`, `--hot-cache-max-size 20%`). So yes — **cache reuse survives across HTTP clients**; dev A's system-prompt prefill warms dev B's request. Known cache bugs: [#1232](https://github.com/jundot/omlx/issues/1232), [#673](https://github.com/jundot/omlx/issues/673), [#825](https://github.com/jundot/omlx/issues/825) (prefix-cache hit breaking tool calling on hybrid-attention models — watch this one).
- **No TLS, no reverse-proxy docs** — bring your own (Tailscale users already do, [#587](https://github.com/jundot/omlx/issues/587)).

**BYO-endpoint config patterns** (full details with URLs from the research pass):

| Tool | base URL | key handling | tiering |
|---|---|---|---|
| aider ([docs](https://aider.chat/docs/llms/openai-compat.html)) | `OPENAI_API_BASE` env / `openai-api-base` in `.aider.conf.yml` (home→repo→cwd, last wins) | plaintext env/yaml | main vs `weak_model_name` |
| llm ([docs](https://llm.datasette.io/en/stable/other-models.html)) | `api_base` **per model** in `extra-openai-models.yaml` | `api_key_name` → `keys.json` indirection | aliases only |
| continue.dev ([reference](https://docs.continue.dev/reference)) | `apiBase` per model in `config.yaml` | `${{ secrets.X }}` / `.env` chain | explicit `roles:` enum |
| goose ([providers](https://goose-docs.ai/docs/getting-started/providers/)) | `OPENAI_HOST`+`OPENAI_BASE_PATH`; custom providers JSON with `base_url` | **`api_key_env` indirection** + OS keychain | `GOOSE_LEAD_MODEL` lead/worker — closest match to think/decide |

Patterns worth stealing: (1) attach `base_url` to the endpoint/model object, not the process; (2) **key indirection** — config names an env var, never holds the secret; (3) tiers are named config (continue's `roles`, goose's lead/worker), exactly like datum's existing `[models]` tiers. SDK note: the official openai SDK reads `OPENAI_BASE_URL`; `OPENAI_API_BASE` is litellm lineage.

**Serving recipes**: `tailscale serve --bg --https=443 localhost:12200` = tailnet-only, automatic Let's Encrypt TLS, `Tailscale-User-Login` identity headers, ACL-restrictable, $0 on the free 3-user plan ([Tailscale Serve KB](https://tailscale.com/kb/1242/tailscale-serve)). Caddy/nginx bearer-check recipes exist (one matcher + `reverse_proxy`; nginx needs `proxy_buffering off` + `proxy_read_timeout 300s` for streaming). LiteLLM proxy gives virtual keys/budgets but needs Postgres and a fat Python service — overkill at 3 users ([their own footprint issue #15262](https://github.com/BerriAI/litellm/issues/15262)). Single static `--api-key` on the inference server itself is the ecosystem norm (llama.cpp `--api-key`, vLLM `VLLM_API_KEY`).

## 3. Proposed design

### Config schema (replaces bare `omlx_url`)

```toml
[inference]                          # the endpoint, not the runtime
backend  = "endpoint"                # "endpoint" | "in-process" | "auto" (default: auto)
base_url = "https://llm-mac.tail1234.ts.net"   # or http://localhost:12200
api_key_env = "DATUM_LLM_API_KEY"    # indirection — config never holds the secret
timeout_s = 300
health_ttl_s = 60                    # cache health probe; don't ping per request

[inference.tiers]                    # converges with [models] reasoning/standard/fast
think  = "mlx-community/Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507-4bit-DWQ"
decide = "mlx-community/gemma-4-E4B-it-qat-4bit"

[local_llm]                          # server-side / in-process-only knobs stay here
enabled = true
hf_cache_dir = "/Volumes/Extra/mlx-models"
kv_bits = 8                          # ignored on endpoint backend
...
```

- **Back-compat shim**: `omlx_url` present → treated as `[inference] base_url` with `backend="auto"`. One deprecation warning.
- **Model profiles** (the per-model TOML proposal) attach to **model IDs, not transport**: `_think`'s `THINK_SAMPLING` (`agent_loop.py:373`) moves into the Qwen profile; profiles resolve identically whether the model is served locally or remotely, since sampling params already travel per-request (`_sampling_params`, `local_llm.py:1513-1529`). For OpenAI/OpenRouter endpoints, a profile simply maps tier→`gpt-4.1-mini` etc. and omits MLX-only keys.
- **Precedence**: CLI flag &gt; env (`DATUM_BASE_URL`, `DATUM_LLM_API_KEY`) &gt; `.datum/config.toml` &gt; defaults — matches aider/goose convention.

### Client mode — code changes

1. Split availability: `endpoint_available()` (reachable, auth'd, cached for `health_ttl_s`) vs `mlx_available()` (current check). `should_use_local()` = either.
2. `chat()` remote path: send `messages` to `/v1/chat/completions` directly; only apply chat template on the in-process path.
3. Add `Authorization: Bearer` header (from `api_key_env`) to `_omlx_available`/`_omlx_generate`/`_omlx_structured`; fix `time_s` (measure wall clock); add one retry with backoff (DPS-200).
4. **Kill the silent local fallback**: if `backend="endpoint"` and the server is down, escalate with a structured reason ("endpoint_unreachable") — never attempt a 19GB local load (DPS-204).
5. `count_tokens` remote path: estimate (len/4) for budgeting; reconcile with `usage.prompt_tokens` from responses.
6. Fix `cli.py:419-426/:458-467` messaging: "no inference backend: set [inference] base_url or pip install datum[local]".

### Packaging

```toml
[project.optional-dependencies]
local  = ["mlx&gt;=0.31", "mlx-lm&gt;=0.31", "outlines&gt;=1.3", "huggingface_hub&gt;=1.16", "tokenizers&gt;=0.22", "numpy&gt;=2"]
memory = ["datum[local]"]   # alias for back-compat (memory_semantic needs mlx too)
```

`pip install datum` = thin client (already true dependency-wise — only the code-path fixes above are needed). `pip install 'datum[local]'` = in-process MLX + embeddings. Lazy imports are already the house style in `local_llm.py`; no new pattern needed.

### Server mode — recipe, not code

**Do not build `datum serve`.** oMLX *is* the server; ship a documented recipe + a `datum doctor`-style check instead:

1. On the 64GB Mac: oMLX with `--api-key` (mandatory anyway once exposed), models **pinned** in the admin panel (prevents LRU-evicting the 19GB Qwen when someone tries a third model), `--max-concurrent-requests 8` default is fine, memory guard default (RAM−8GB) is fine for 19GB+5GB.
2. Keep oMLX bound to **localhost**; expose via `tailscale serve --bg --https=443 localhost:12200`. Tailnet membership + ACL + identity headers + auto-TLS, zero new daemons, $0 for 3 users. LAN-without-Tailscale fallback: 0.0.0.0 bind + mandatory API key (plaintext HTTP — acceptable on a trusted home/office LAN, say so explicitly in the doc).
3. Each dev: `export DATUM_LLM_API_KEY=…`, `base_url = "https://llm-mac.&lt;tailnet&gt;.ts.net"`. Done.
4. Skip Caddy/nginx (oMLX self-auths), skip LiteLLM (Postgres + fat service for budgets nobody asked for).

### Security placement (S0-S3 interaction)

S0 token-stripping / rules demotion / salted tags are **client-side by necessity** — they harden prompt *assembly*, which happens in `agent_loop.py`/`tdd_driver.py` before any HTTP call. The server never sees untrusted-vs-trusted structure; it just completes. Two additions for shared-box mode: (a) treat the bearer key as a real secret (it gates a machine that runs `run_command` payloads downstream — prompt-injection blast radius now spans three devs' repos); (b) S2.1's network-deny sandbox must allowlist the inference endpoint host.

## 4. Risks

- **Concurrency**: real but mild. Continuous batching means two devs share decode throughput (~halved tok/s each, no head-of-line blocking up to 8 requests). The chatty ReAct loop makes many short calls, which batches well. Worst case is two simultaneous long `act_green` prefills — slow, not broken.
- **Prompt cache on shared box**: actually a *benefit* — content-keyed global prefix cache means shared system prompts prefill once. Risk: cross-tenant prefix-cache timing is an info-leak channel in theory; irrelevant for 3 trusted teammates. Watch oMLX [#825](https://github.com/jundot/omlx/issues/825) (cache hit breaking tool calling) — pin oMLX versions.
- **Latency**: ReAct loop does ~10-30 HTTP round trips/phase; tailnet RTT (~1-20ms) is noise vs multi-second decodes. The per-call 1s health probe is the real overhead — fix with the TTL cache.
- **Silent fallback** (`local_llm.py:218`): today a down server triggers a local model load; on a remote client this becomes an exception mid-pipeline. Must become a structured escalation before anything ships.
- **Memory eviction**: a teammate loading a third model can evict the pinned pair — pinning + a "models to pin" line in the recipe handles it.

## 5. Sanity check + phased plan (smallest slice first)

**Is it worth it?** Yes, but smaller than it sounds. The dependency split already exists; oMLX already does auth, batching, and multi-client caching; Tailscale already does transport. The entire project is ~150 LOC of client fixes + a config schema + a one-page recipe. What's NOT worth building: `datum serve`, a custom proxy, LiteLLM, virtual keys, request queueing — all either exist upstream or solve 30-dev problems for a 3-dev team.

- **Phase 1 (half day) — make remote work at all**: `[inference]` schema + `omlx_url` shim; bearer header; health-probe TTL; kill silent fallback; fix `chat()` remote path and `is_available()` gating; fix CLI messages. Test against localhost oMLX with a key set.
- **Phase 2 (half day) — packaging + docs**: `local` extra (alias `memory`); README "thin client" install; the Tailscale-serve server recipe; `datum local-llm` status shows endpoint/auth/latency.
- **Phase 3 (one day, after profile proposal lands) — provider generality**: tier→model mapping per profile; verify against one non-oMLX endpoint (OpenRouter or Ollama) — mainly confirming `response_format: json_schema` degradation paths (some providers lack strict mode → fall back to two-pass extraction, which datum already has).
- **Phase 4 (deferred)**: remote embeddings for `memory_semantic.py`; concurrency telemetry; only revisit LiteLLM if the team grows.

One flag per the see-something rule: the hardcoded `time_s: 0.0` at `local_llm.py:1571,1620` silently zeroes the tokens/sec metrics and `--stats` savings math for every oMLX call today — worth filing/fixing regardless of this split.</result>

# datum-share: MCP Server Design for Cross-Machine datum Queries over Tailscale

## 1. State inventory — what's queryable on each machine

| Asset | Path | Contents | Default share posture |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pipeline state | `.datum/state.json` (mirror of `.datum/state.db`, table `kv_state`) | `run_id`, `current_phase`, `phases{status, started_at, completed_at}`, `lanes{stage, stages.RED/GREEN/REFACTOR, files_touched, depends_on, blocked_on_*}`, `git{base_branch, work_branch}`, `brief_defects[]` | Share (filtered) |
| Phase list | `datum/state.py` PHASES | discovery, refine, plan, prior_art, triage, deepen, properties, act, validate, review, pr_comments, closeout | — |
| Epic docs | `docs/epics/datum/epic-&lt;N&gt;/` | `SPEC.md`, `TICKET.md`, `QUESTIONS.md`, `TASKS.md`, `tasks.json` (id/title/acceptance_criteria/files/depends_on), `PROPERTIES.md`, optional `RETRO.md`, `WALKTHROUGH.md` | Share SPEC/TICKET/TASKS; rest opt-in |
| Orientation | `docs/CURRENT_STATE.md` | shipped / in-flight / next | Share |
| Lane plan | `.datum/lane-plan.json` | topological lane order, file ownership — answers "who's touching what" | Share (filtered) |
| Run artifacts | `.datum/runs/&lt;RUN_ID&gt;/` | `closeout-data.json` (commits, files, LOC), `closeout-raw/*.json`, `RETRO.md`, `follow-ups.json`, adversarial findings | **Deny** (commit messages, raw reviewer output = leak surface) |
| Review reports | `REVIEW-REPORT.md` via `datum/render.py` | findings with file:line | Opt-in |
| LLM metrics | `.datum/local-llm-metrics.jsonl`, `token_metrics` table | model IDs, throughput | **Deny** (infra fingerprinting) |
| Config | `.datum/config.toml` | model tiers, phase routing | **Deny always** (hard-coded, not overridable) |
| Existing sanitizer | `datum/shared/logging/sanitizer.py` | redacts `wfc_`/`sk_` keys, bearer/JWT tokens, escapes control chars | Reuse in redaction pipeline |
| S0–S3 security plan | `/Volumes/Extra/repos/gitrdunhq/datum-local/docs/security-upgrade-plan-20260610-213924.md` + `prompt-injection-security-20260610-213347.md` | special-token strip, salted tags, invisible-Unicode strip, "consequence layer beats prompt layer" doctrine | Reuse S0.1 sanitizer on all outbound content |

## 2. Prior art (URLs)

**MCP spec (current state, June 2026):** stable spec is **2025-11-25** — Streamable HTTP transport, OAuth 2.1 authorization where a protected MCP server is an **OAuth 2.1 resource server**. The 2026-07-28 RC (locked, publishing next month) goes stateless: no `initialize` handshake, no `Mcp-Session-Id`, plus six authorization-hardening SEPs (SEP-2468 `iss` validation per RFC 9207, SEP-2352 credential-issuer binding, etc.). Design stateless from day one.
- Spec authorization: https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/draft/basic/authorization
- 2026-07-28 RC: https://blog.modelcontextprotocol.io/posts/2026-07-28-release-candidate/

**Tailscale's MCP work** (this is the centerpiece):
- **tsidp** — OIDC/OAuth IdP for your tailnet; explicitly supports the MCP authorization spec including Dynamic Client Registration: https://github.com/tailscale/tsidp · docs: https://tailscale.com/docs/features/tsidp
- Blog: "How Tailscale can make secure MCP access with DCR easier": https://tailscale.com/blog/dynamic-client-registration-dcr-for-mcp-ai — key insight: every tailnet request arrives with SSO-backed user identity attached; the MCP server can check it directly, or tsidp can mint spec-compliant OAuth tokens from it.
- Blog: "Zero trust with zero clicks": https://tailscale.com/blog/zero-trust-with-zero-clicks-a-new-take-on-idps · "Building tsidp": https://tailscale.com/blog/building-tsidp
- **Reference implementation**: https://github.com/remyguercio/tailscale-mcp-echo — Python + FastMCP behind `tailscale serve`, which injects `Tailscale-User-Login` identity headers into proxied requests. This is exactly our shape.

**Project-state MCP prior art:**
- GitHub MCP server (remote, OAuth-protected, repo/issue/PR state): https://github.com/github/github-mcp-server
- Filesystem MCP server (roots-scoped, allowlisted directories — the path-confinement pattern): https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/servers/tree/main/src/filesystem
- Python SDK / FastMCP (streamable HTTP + `TokenVerifier` for resource-server auth): https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/python-sdk · https://github.com/jlowin/fastmcp

## 3. Threat model

**Actors:** (a) honest-but-curious teammate — sees more than owner intended; (b) compromised teammate device — full MCP client capability with valid tailnet identity; (c) non-team tailnet node (shared node, exit node, future contractor); (d) malicious or poisoned repo content flowing *through* the server to the querier's LLM.

**Assets:** WIP code and specs (competitively/contractually sensitive), secrets accidentally in run artifacts (env dumps, tokens in pytest output), commit messages, infra details (model stack, machine specs), the owner's filesystem beyond the repo.

**Top risks and mitigations:**
1. **Path traversal / symlink escape** → resolve-then-confine: `Path.resolve()`, `relative_to(repo_root)` check, reject symlinks, evaluate sharing rules on the *resolved* relative path. Same pattern as datum's SAFE-001 write-tool sandbox, inverted for reads.
2. **Over-sharing via misconfig** → default-deny: no `sharing.toml` = serve nothing; deny rules always beat share rules; `config`, `*.env`, `.git/`, anything outside repo root are hard-coded denies that no TOML can override.
3. **Identity spoofing** → server binds `127.0.0.1` only; tailnet exposure exclusively via `tailscale serve` (which terminates TLS and injects identity headers signed by the local tailscaled). Requests without `Tailscale-User-Login` are rejected. Tailnet ACLs additionally restrict the port to `group:datum-team`.
4. **Prompt injection of the QUERIER** — the inversion of datum-local's S0 threat model: shared SPEC/TICKET/state content is *untrusted input to the teammate's LLM*. A poisoned epic doc on Sam's machine becomes an injection vector into Alice's Claude. Mitigation, server-side (consequence layer, per the S0 doctrine): run all outbound content through S0.1-style sanitization — strip chat-template special tokens (`&lt;|im_start|&gt;`, `&lt;start_of_turn&gt;`, `&lt;bos&gt;`…), strip invisible Unicode (zero-width, bidi, PUA), plus `sanitizer.py` secret redaction. Every response carries `"provenance": {"owner": "...", "trust": "untrusted-peer-content"}` so querier-side agents can wrap it accordingly. Document loudly that the querier's client must treat results as data, not instructions.
5. **Mutation / RCE** → no write tools exist, period; no subprocess, no `eval`, no shell anywhere in the server (datum's SEC-002 predicate applies verbatim); state.db opened `file:...?mode=ro`. Read-only is structural, not behavioral.
6. **Exfil via artifacts** → `runs/**` and metrics denied by default; per-response byte cap; per-peer rate limit.
7. **No accountability** → append-only audit log (below).

## 4. AuthN: tailnet identity, two stages

**Stage A (ship first) — `tailscale serve` identity headers.** Server binds loopback; `tailscale serve --bg --set-path /mcp 8841` publishes it as `https://&lt;machine&gt;.&lt;tailnet&gt;.ts.net/mcp`. Tailscale injects `Tailscale-User-Login` / `Tailscale-User-Name` on every proxied request — cryptographically tied to the WireGuard tunnel, no passwords, no bearer tokens to leak. The server reads the login, maps it to a peer policy, denies unknowns. Claude Code connects via `claude mcp add --transport http`; clients lacking remote support use `mcp-remote`.

**Stage B — tsidp as OAuth 2.1 authorization server.** For spec-conformant MCP authorization (and clients that demand the OAuth flow): run tsidp on the tailnet; datum-share becomes a resource server validating tsidp-issued JWTs via a FastMCP `TokenVerifier` (JWKS from tsidp, `iss`/`aud` checks per SEP-2468/2352). tsidp supports DCR so MCP clients self-register. Identity claim (`email` = tailnet login) feeds the same peer-policy layer — authZ code is identical across stages.

## 5. AuthZ: `.datum/sharing.toml` (default-deny)

House style matches `.datum/config.toml` (snake_case, `# ──` separators, nested tables):

```toml
# ── datum sharing policy ─────────────────────────────────
# Default-deny: absent file or unmatched path = not shared.
schema_version = "1.0"

[defaults]
policy = "deny"                  # only "deny" is valid; field exists for self-documentation
redact = true                    # outbound sanitization always on

# Logical resources + repo-relative path globs (evaluated post-resolve)
share = [
  "state",                       # filtered .datum/state.json view
  "current_state",               # docs/CURRENT_STATE.md
  "lane_plan",                   # filtered .datum/lane-plan.json
  "epics/*/SPEC.md",
  "epics/*/TICKET.md",
  "epics/*/TASKS.md",
]

# Deny ALWAYS wins over share, at every level.
deny = [
  "runs/**",
  "metrics",
  "**/*.secret", "**/*.env", "**/.env*",
  "epics/*/QUESTIONS.md",
]
# Hard-coded, non-overridable denies (in code, not here):
#   .datum/config.toml, .git/**, anything outside repo root, symlinks

[redaction]
strip_env_assignments = true     # KEY=value lines for *_TOKEN, *_KEY, *_SECRET, AWS_*, GH*
strip_special_tokens = true      # chat-template tokens, S0.1
strip_invisible_unicode = true   # zero-width, bidi, PUA
extra_patterns = ["ghp_[A-Za-z0-9]{20,}", "(?i)password\\s*[:=]"]
max_bytes_per_response = 65536

[limits]
requests_per_minute_per_peer = 30

# ── per-teammate overrides ──────────────────────────────
[peers."alice@github"]
share = ["epics/epic-27/**", "review_reports"]   # additive grants
deny  = []

[peers."bob@github"]
deny = ["epics/epic-30/**"]                      # subtractive

# Unknown tailnet identities: no [peers] entry = denied entirely.
require_known_peer = true
```

Resolution: hard-coded deny → peer deny → global deny → peer share → global share → deny. The loader is pure (DPS-101): `(peer, resource_path) -&gt; Allow|Deny(reason)`, trivially property-testable.

## 6. MCP surface (5 tools + resources)

Tools (primary — best client support); all read-only, all return `provenance`:

1. **`get_pipeline_status()`** → filtered state: `run_id`, `current_phase`, per-phase status/timestamps, lane summary (id, title, stage, files_touched, blocked_on), work branch. Strips `config_hash`, model fields, SHAs.
2. **`list_epics()`** → epic numbers, titles (from TICKET first line), status (active per state.json vs closed), which docs are shared with *this* peer.
3. **`get_epic_doc(epic, doc)`** → one of SPEC|TICKET|TASKS|PROPERTIES|tasks.json|RETRO, policy-gated, redacted, size-capped.
4. **`search_shared_docs(query, scope?)`** → literal/regex search over the peer's *allowed* path set only (pure-Python scan, no shell), returns path + line + snippet.
5. **`get_recent_activity(path_glob?)`** → "who's touched what": lanes' `files_touched` + lane-plan ownership, filtered by glob. Answers "who's on the auth module" — querier fans out to all three machines and merges client-side.

Resources (mirror, for resource-aware clients): `datum://state`, `datum://current-state`, `datum://epics/{n}/{doc}` with `ttlMs` caching hints per the new spec. `get_current_state()` rides as resource + tool. No prompts. Nothing else — small surface, small attack surface.

## 7. Audit log

`.datum/sharing-audit.jsonl`, append-only, one record per request:
`{"ts": ISO8601, "peer": "alice@github", "node": "alice-mbp", "tool": "get_epic_doc", "args": {...}, "decision": "allow|deny", "deny_reason": "...", "paths": [...], "bytes": 1234}` — written through the existing sanitized JSON formatter. Surfaced later as `datum share audit [--peer X --since Y]` (docs say `datum &lt;command&gt;`, never scripts).

## 8. Phased plan (smallest useful slice first)

- **P0 — Local slice (~1 day):** new `datum/share/` package — `policy.py` (TOML loader + pure decision function), `paths.py` (resolve-and-confine), `server.py` (FastMCP, streamable HTTP, loopback), tools 1 + `get_current_state`, audit log. `datum share serve` / `datum share status` CLI. TDD: policy property tests (default-deny, deny-beats-share, traversal/symlink corpus) red-first.
- **P1 — Tailnet + identity (~half day):** `tailscale serve` wiring + setup doc, `Tailscale-User-Login` extraction, `require_known_peer`, per-peer overrides, rate limit, tailnet ACL snippet for `group:datum-team`.
- **P2 — Redaction + full surface (~1 day):** redaction pipeline (reuse `sanitizer.py` + port S0.1 special-token/Unicode strip from datum-local), tools 2–5, resources, byte caps. Red-team fixture: hostile SPEC.md with injection payloads → assert stripped/flagged (mirrors the S0 fixture pattern).
- **P3 — tsidp OAuth (~1 day, optional until a client forces it):** tsidp deployment, FastMCP `TokenVerifier` against tsidp JWKS, protected-resource metadata, DCR. AuthZ layer unchanged.
- **P4 — Team aggregation (client-side):** a skill/agent that queries all three servers and merges — never a central server; the data stays where it lives.

Dependencies: `mcp` (or `fastmcp`) is the only new package; everything else is stdlib (`tomllib`, `fnmatch`, `sqlite3`, `json`, `hashlib`). What's mine is yours — terms and conditions in `sharing.toml`.

**What changed**

Two files modified, both on branch `fix/write-echo-truncation`. Commit `881db81`.

**`datum/local_llm.py`** (3 fixes, +62 lines / -4 lines):

1. **Retry with capped backoff (DPS-200).** New `_omlx_urlopen_with_retry` helper (lines 1538-1581). Retries on HTTP 429, 503, and `URLError(ConnectionRefusedError)` with jittered exponential backoff (base 2^attempt + uniform jitter). Honors `Retry-After` response header when present. Caps at 4 attempts. Non-retryable HTTP errors (400, 401, 500, etc.) raise immediately. After exhaustion, re-raises the original error so the existing escalation ladder fires. Both `_omlx_generate` and `_omlx_structured` now use this helper instead of bare `urlopen`.

2. **Config-driven timeout.** Added `"request_timeout_s": 300` to `DEFAULTS` dict (line 39). Both functions read `load_config().get("request_timeout_s", 300)` instead of hardcoded `timeout=300`. Follows the same `DEFAULTS` merge pattern as all other config values.

3. **Real `time_s`.** Both functions wrap the HTTP call with `time.monotonic()` and return the elapsed seconds instead of hardcoded `0.0`. This fixes zeroed tokens/sec metrics for every oMLX call.

**`tests/test_local_llm_hardening.py`** — 14 new tests added (total now 24):

**Code-quality advisory for the oMLX path (lines ~1488-1680)**

Observations only -- no changes made beyond the three scoped fixes.

1. **[Severity: Medium] Structural duplication between `_omlx_generate` and `_omlx_structured` (lines 1584-1630 vs 1634-1680).** Both functions build an identical `urllib.request.Request`, call `load_config()` for timeout, wrap `_omlx_urlopen_with_retry`, parse the JSON response, and extract from `data["choices"][0]["message"]["content"]`. The only differences are: the request body shape (structured adds `response_format`), the response parsing (structured does `json.loads(raw)`), and the return dict keys. A shared `_omlx_call(body, url, timeout_s)` -&gt; `(data, elapsed)` helper would eliminate ~20 duplicated lines and ensure future fixes (auth headers, logging, etc.) only need one touch point.

2. **[Severity: Medium] Double `load_config()` call per request (lines 1614+1488).** Both functions call `load_config()` to get `request_timeout_s`. The callers of these functions (likely in the pipeline layer) probably already have config loaded. Accepting `timeout_s` as a parameter with a default would avoid redundant TOML parsing on every request and make the functions easier to test without patching `load_config`.

3. **[Severity: Medium] Silent fallback in `_omlx_available` (lines 1492-1508, DPS-204).** When the health check fails (connection refused, DNS error, timeout), the function returns `False` silently. No log, no structured error. The pipeline then falls back to the local MLX path (or cloud API), and the operator has no signal that their oMLX server is down. At minimum this should `structlog.get_logger().warning("omlx_health_check_failed", url=url, error=str(e))`.

4. **[Severity: Low] No boundary validation on oMLX response shape (DPS-102, lines 1615-1616 / 1669-1670).** Both functions blindly index `data["choices"][0]["message"]["content"]` without validating the response shape. A malformed response (missing `choices`, empty array, missing `content` key) produces an opaque `KeyError`/`IndexError` instead of a structured error with context (which model, what URL, what was returned). A guard like `if not data.get("choices"): raise ValueError(...)` would be a small improvement.

5. **[Severity: Low] `_omlx_structured` doesn't validate parsed JSON against the schema (line 1670).** The function requests `"strict": True` grammar-constrained generation, then does `parsed = json.loads(raw)` and returns the dict without checking it against the Pydantic `schema`. If the server ignores `strict` or returns malformed JSON that parses but doesn't match the schema, the error surfaces much later in the pipeline. A `schema.model_validate(parsed)` call here would fail fast at the boundary.

6. **[Severity: Low] Testability of retry timing.** The `_omlx_urlopen_with_retry` helper imports `random` internally and uses `time.sleep` from the module-level `time` import. Tests patch `time.sleep` at the `time.sleep` path (which works because the module does `import time` and calls `time.sleep`). This is fine, but if someone later changes the import to `from time import sleep`, the patches break silently. Accepting a `sleep_fn` parameter (defaulting to `time.sleep`) would make the retry helper fully deterministic in tests without fragile patching.</result>

## Key prompt-engineering findings

1. **TOML basic strings are a trap for this task.** s0-1 used `"""` strings, but TOML basic strings decode `\uXXXX` at parse time — they would inject raw invisibles straight into the prompts. Both new specs use TOML *literal* strings (`'''`) so the escapes stay ASCII; the s0-2a header documents this so nobody "fixes" it back.
2. **The hazard bit me in real time, proving the spec's point.** My first Write of s0-2a went through JSON decoding that converted `\u200b` etc. into actual raw invisible characters (confirmed by byte scan: E2 80 8B, EF BB BF, PUA bytes). I fixed it deterministically with a small script (`.temp/fix_escapes.py`, gitignored) that rewrites raw invisibles back to ASCII `\uXXXX` text — the invisible-character version of Gretchen trying to make "fetch" happen. It does not survive.

- Both files: `tomllib.load` OK and `load_task_spec()` OK (names `strip-invisible-unicode` / `hash-pin-rules` resolved, commit messages correct).

<result>Perfect. Now I'll write the final report:

## Report: Preventing Parallel-Agent Merge Conflicts

### Root Causes

1. **No pre-dispatch overlap detection**: Agents #61 and #65 were dispatched in parallel despite both modifying the same file (`datum/local_llm.py`) and the same three functions (`_omlx_urlopen_with_retry`, `_omlx_generate`, `_omlx_structured`). The dispatcher had no mechanism to detect file-level collisions before launch.

2. **Uncontrolled base selection**: When agents discovered they lacked prerequisite commits, each self-upgraded its worktree base independently. Agent #61 reset to commit 881db81; agent #65 fast-forwarded to bcd2d07. Neither informed the orchestrator. This diverged their merge bases and produced a 13-hunk conflict on subsequent merge.

3. **No structured prerequisite reporting**: Agents treated missing prerequisites as a problem to solve locally (by adjusting their base) rather than as intelligence to report. The orchestrator never learned they had dependencies and couldn't re-plan accordingly.

---

### Recommended Dispatch Protocol

**Apply this at every multi-agent dispatch:**

**Pre-Dispatch Phase:**
1. Extract target files from each GitHub issue (from body, suggested fix, or by searching the codebase).
2. Build a file-to-issue map and detect overlaps. Files touched by 2+ issues → serialize those agents (chain: second agent branches from first's result), not parallel.
3. All non-chained agents start from a single explicit base commit (e.g., `main` tip). Do NOT allow agents to self-upgrade.
4. Document the dispatch decision: note which agents are parallel vs. chained, and the base commit for each group.

**Agent Execution Phase:**
5. After worktree checkout, if an agent detects a missing prerequisite (commit X not in its history), it must **STOP and report** to the orchestrator. Do not self-upgrade.
6. Include a structured "prerequisites discovered" field in the agent's result so the orchestrator can re-plan if needed.

**Merge-Integration Phase:**
7. Collect agent results in dependency order. If agent A and agent B both touch file X, merge A first, then B (B may auto-rebase onto A's commit).
8. Validate post-merge: run `git diff HEAD~1 --name-only` for each merge to confirm no unexpected files were modified.

---

### Steal from lane_plan.py

The datum pipeline (`datum/lane_plan.py`) already solves this for Act lanes:

- **`build_file_ownership()`** (lines 109–123): detects which files are claimed by which lanes and flags conflicts.
- **`topological_sort()`** (lines 126–151): outputs a safe execution order respecting declared `depends_on` links.
- **`write_conflicts` metadata** (line 278): each lane knows which other lanes touch its files.

**For ad-hoc agent dispatch**, reuse the same pattern:
1. Model each GitHub issue as a "lane" with a `files` list and `depends_on` list.
2. Call `build_file_ownership(issues)` before dispatch to detect conflicts.
3. If conflicts exist, add explicit `depends_on` edges and call `topological_sort()` to compute chaining order.
4. Use the sorted order for both dispatch and merge integration.

---

### One-Liner Summary

## Report

### Defect 1: Leading `"""` eaten from written file

**Root cause confirmed: fence pairing regex.** The regex `_FENCE_RE = re.compile(r"```[^\n]*\n(.*?)```", re.DOTALL)` consumed the entire opening-fence line via `[^\n]*\n`, treating everything after the backticks as an info-string. When the model placed the opening `"""` on the same line as the fence (e.g., `` ```python """ ``), the `"""` was eaten as part of the info-string, and the captured group started after the newline -- missing the docstring opener.

**Evidence:** Exact reproduction:
```python
thought = '```python """\nTest cases for strip_special_tokens function."""\n```'
# OLD regex captures: 'Test cases for strip_special_tokens function."""\n'
# Missing the opening """
```

**Server channel ruled out:** Raw probe to oMLX at localhost:12200 confirmed triple-quotes survive round-trip intact.

**Fix:** Changed `_FENCE_RE` to `re.compile(r"```\w*[ \t]*\n?(.*?)```", re.DOTALL)` -- only consumes the language-tag word chars, makes the newline optional, and captures everything else.

### Defect 2a: Syntactically invalid .py writes accepted

**Fix in `/Volumes/Extra/repos/gitrdunhq/datum/datum/agent_loop.py`:** Added `_check_py_syntax()` helper returning the SyntaxError or None. Added an `elif` guard in the tool-dispatch chain that rejects `.py` writes failing `ast.parse` BEFORE `_execute_tool` runs. Returns an error observation with line number and message. Existing `_lint_python` warnings (eval, os.system, etc.) continue to fire post-write for valid-but-questionable Python.

### Defect 2b: SyntaxError accepted as valid RED

**Fix in `/Volumes/Extra/repos/gitrdunhq/datum-local/datum_local/tdd_driver.py`:** Added `classify_red_result(stdout, target_module) -&gt; RedClassification` with typed result. SyntaxError is always rejected at the driver level (unconditional `re.search` before classify). For specs with a `from X import` pattern, deeper classification runs: accepts `N failed`, accepts ImportError/NameError on the target module, rejects unrelated collection errors. Specs without a detectable target module fall back to the old "any non-zero returncode" behavior.

### Defect 3: Empty content raises instead of retrying

**Fix in `/Volumes/Extra/repos/gitrdunhq/datum/datum/local_llm.py`:** `_omlx_call` now wraps its request in a retry loop (up to `_OMLX_MAX_ATTEMPTS`). A 200 response with content that is None or empty string triggers exponential backoff and retry. Honors the existing `deadline` parameter. Only raises `ValueError` after all retries exhausted. Missing/empty `choices` still raises immediately (not retryable -- structural error).

### RED retry loop behavior

**Max model episodes:** 2 (initial + 1 retry). No second retry -- if the retry also fails, `tdd-failure.json` is written and exit 1.

**Corrective prompt on retry contains:**
- "YOUR PREVIOUS RED ATTEMPT WAS REJECTED" header
- The rejection reason (e.g. "RED deleted previously-passing tests: baseline 17 passed, now 0 passed")
- Truncated pytest output (first 800 chars)
- Explicit instruction: "your last write DELETED previously-passing tests -- re-emit the COMPLETE file: ALL existing tests preserved verbatim PLUS the new failing tests"
- The original `spec.red_prompt` appended at the end

**Verification flow (per attempt):**
1. Check target files exist
2. Run scoped pytest
3. Reject if returncode == 0 (tests shouldn't pass in RED)
4. Reject if SyntaxError in output
5. Classify failure pattern (if target_module extractable)
6. **NEW:** Compare `parse_passed_count(stdout)` against baseline -- reject if post &lt; baseline

### Fix 3 detail

`_check_py_syntax` was called twice on the syntax-error rejection path (once in the `elif` condition at line 635, again at line 640 to get the `SyntaxError` object). Now uses a walrus operator `(se := _check_py_syntax(...))` to call once and bind the result.

### Fix 2 detail

`_detect_removed_defs(old_content, new_content)` AST-parses both and returns sorted list of top-level function/class names present in old but absent in new. If either file doesn't parse, returns `[]` (skip comparison, no crash). The old content is snapshot from disk before `_execute_tool` runs. Warning appears in the observation (not a rejection), name list truncated at 10 with "+N more" suffix.

### Anything still broken (severity &gt;= moderate)

Nothing new found. The existing `test_tdd_driver_e2e.py::test_tdd_driver_red_green` failure in datum-local is the known model-dependent flake (it runs a live model).</result>

### Post-RED decision table as implemented

| # | Input shape | Verdict | Mechanism |
|---|-------------|---------|-----------|
| 1 | SyntaxError in pytest output | REJECT | Regex match, unchanged |
| 2 | Baseline test names missing from AST of post-RED file | REJECT | `_check_baseline_names_preserved()` -- lists up to 10 missing names |
| 3 | ImportError/NameError on target module (all baseline names preserved) | ACCEPT | `classify_red_result` + AST check passed; passed-count comparison skipped |
| 4 | Tests ran with "N failed" (all baseline names preserved) | ACCEPT | Passed-count &gt;= baseline check still applies on this branch |
| 5 | Unrelated collection error | REJECT | `classify_red_result` returns `valid_red=False` |
| 6 | No recognized failure pattern | REJECT | Fallthrough in `classify_red_result` |

### 3. Exact new fence semantics and syntax-gate message format

**Fence regex**: `r"```[^\n]*\n(.*?)```"` (standard Markdown). Everything after ` ``` ` to end of line is the info string and is discarded. Content capture starts on the next line. The a4d815e Defect-1 case (content on the fence line) is now caught by the ast syntax gate, which rejects it before disk and gives the model feedback to re-emit on the next line.

**Syntax-gate message format** (when error is at line 1):
```
Error: file was NOT written to disk — Python syntax error at line 1: invalid syntax. Captured line 1 was: 'tests/test_prompt_sanitizer.py"""Test cases."""'. Re-emit the full corrected file in one fenced code block and retry write_to_file.
```
The `Captured line 1 was: &lt;repr&gt;` clause appears only when `se.lineno == 1`, truncated to 120 chars.

**Idempotent-write observation**:
```
File already contains exactly this content — write skipped, nothing changed. The file is complete; DO NOT write it again. Proceed to the next action or call done.
```
The path is added to `read_paths` (counts as a successful read). No tool execution occurs. Textually distinct from a normal write echo.

### 4. Driver behavior matrix on escalation

| Escalation reason | Verify accept | Verify reject |
|---|---|---|
| `loop_detected` | Log "RED/GREEN verified despite escalation (loop_detected)", continue pipeline | Fail immediately with combined reason, no retry |
| `exception: ...` | Log "verified despite escalation", continue | Fail immediately, no retry |
| `budget_exceeded` / `timeout_exceeded` / `max_steps_exhausted` | Log "verified despite escalation", continue | Fail immediately, no retry |

Key design choice: escalation + rejection skips retry (the agent already gave up, retrying the same prompt won't help). Non-escalated rejections still get 1 retry as before.

### 5. Anything still broken (severity &gt;= moderate) out of scope

Nothing new found. The three fixes are self-contained and don't surface any adjacent issues. The 2 datum-local e2e test flakes (`test_tdd_driver_red_green`, `test_failure_record_on_bad_fixture`) are pre-existing live-model tests that require a running oMLX server -- not affected by these changes.</result>

### 3. Transcript path + per-line schema
Path: `&lt;target repo root&gt;/.datum/transcripts/&lt;YYYYMMDDTHHMMSSZ&gt;-&lt;episode&gt;.jsonl` — UTC timestamp captured once at episode start; `episode` is the loop's `phase` arg (`act_red`, `act_green`, …). Written relative to cwd, which the driver chdirs to the fixture dir, so it lands under the target repo.

Per line:
```json
{"step": 0, "episode": "act_green",
 "think_raw": "&lt;full think response BEFORE think-tag stripping&gt;",
 "decide_raw": {"action": "tool", "tool_name": "...", "tool_args": {...}},
 "tool_name": "run_command",
 "tool_args": {"path": "...", "content": "&lt;truncated at 500 + ...[truncated]&gt;"},
 "observation": "&lt;truncated at 1000 + ...[truncated]&gt;"}
```
Pseudo-steps `done` and `truncated_thought` are also logged (`decide_raw` is `{}` for truncated thoughts). All writes wrapped in `try/except OSError` — a blocked `.datum/transcripts` path (tested) degrades to no-op, never crashes the loop.

### 4. Breaker ordering relative to loop detector
The breaker sits immediately after the observation is finalized and **before** the step is appended to history/steps_log, so the corrective text reaches the model's next THINK prompt. Sequence per step: assemble → execute → finalize observation → **breaker check** (current `(tool, args)` signature == previous step's AND current observation == previous observation, `_no_progress_fired` once-per-episode flag) → append/on_step → transcript log → existing loop detector (3 identical signatures). Net effect: corrective injected on the 2nd identical step; detector still escalates `loop_detected` on the 3rd — both verified by test.

Note one deliberate divergence from RED in Change 3: GREEN retries even when the loop escalated (RED fails immediately on escalate+reject). That's per spec — run-6's GREEN escalated via loop detection without writing, which is exactly the case the corrective prompt targets. `_result_wrote_file()` ignores write steps whose observation starts with `"Error"` (rejected writes never touched disk).

Not filed as a GitHub issue per the local-only git-ops rule; flagging here instead.
- **Minor (low):** transcript filename has 1-second resolution — two episodes of the same phase starting within the same second append to one file. Lines carry `episode` and `step` so it stays parseable; worth a PID or counter suffix if parallel episodes ever happen.</result>

## Report

### 1. Commit SHA + Files

**Commit**: `27d8601` on `master`

Files:
- `/Volumes/Extra/repos/gitrdunhq/datum-local/specs/s0-3-pytest-verdict.toml` (NEW) -- task spec for parse_pytest_verdict TDD cycle
- `/Volumes/Extra/repos/gitrdunhq/datum-local/datum_local/tdd_driver.py` (MODIFIED) -- added `ModuleNotFoundError` to RED classification regex
- `/Volumes/Extra/repos/gitrdunhq/datum-local/tests/test_tdd_driver_verification.py` (MODIFIED) -- 2 new tests for ModuleNotFoundError classification

### 2. classify_red_result DID need the ModuleNotFoundError extension

The existing regex `r"(ImportError|NameError)"` only matched `ImportError` and `NameError` by literal text in stdout. When the entire module is absent (S0.3 shape), Python raises `ModuleNotFoundError` and pytest prints that exact class name. With `--tb=short`, pytest also prints `ImportError while importing test module` as a wrapper line, which incidentally matches -- but this is fragile and depends on the traceback format.

**What changed**: Both regex patterns in `classify_red_result` (target-module match at line 127 and unrelated-error match at line 134) were extended from `(ImportError|NameError)` to `(ImportError|ModuleNotFoundError|NameError)`. Docstrings updated to match.

The test I wrote for RED confirmation: stdout containing only `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'datum_local.pytest_verdict'` (without any `ImportError` wrapper text) failed classification before the fix, passed after.

### 3. Test counts

- **Before**: 106 unit tests passing (108 collected total including 2 skipped e2e)
- **After**: 108 unit tests passing (110 collected total including 2 skipped e2e)
- **Delta**: +2 tests (`test_classify_red_result_module_not_found_error_on_target_is_valid`, `test_classify_red_result_module_not_found_error_unrelated_is_invalid`)

### 4. Import line and target module extraction

- **Spec mandates import**: `from datum_local.pytest_verdict import parse_pytest_verdict`
- **`_extract_target_module` derives**: `datum_local.pytest_verdict`
- **ModuleNotFoundError message will contain**: `No module named 'datum_local.pytest_verdict'`
- **`target_module in stdout` check**: matches (confirmed programmatically)

## Assessment: agents-best-practices vs datum/datum-local

### 1. What the repo is

A curated, provider-neutral best-practices skill for agentic harness design. MIT license. ~1.9k stars, 7 commits, 1 contributor (DenisSergeevitch). It is a markdown-only knowledge base -- no executable code, no harness implementation. 16 reference docs covering loop invariants, tool design, permissions, planning, context compaction, cost/caching, workflow orchestration, coding-agent overlays, security/evals, and checklists. Quality is high -- the writing is precise, structural, and practice-oriented, not hand-wavy. But it is purely advisory: it describes what a harness should do, not how to build one.

### 2. GRAB list (ranked by value for datum/datum-local)

**GRAB-1: Structured tool-result format with `next_valid_actions`** (`tools-and-permissions.md` lines 170-198)
The repo specifies returning `{"status": "success|error", "summary": "...", "next_valid_actions": [...]}` from every tool. datum's `_execute_tool` returns raw strings. Adding a `next_valid_actions` field to tool results would give the THINK model a narrower action space after each step -- particularly valuable for a 30B model that drifts.
**Lands in:** `agent_loop.py` observation construction, `lane_tools_runner.py` result format.

**GRAB-2: Typed event log replacing/augmenting the step dict** (`architecture.md` lines 96-119)
The repo defines typed events: `tool_call`, `tool_result`, `approval_request`, `plan_update`, `context_compaction`, `error`, `final_answer`. datum's `steps_log` is untyped dicts. Switching to typed events (even just a `Literal` field on each step dict) improves transcript analysis, replay, and the future M2 orchestrator's ability to route.
**Lands in:** `agent_loop.py` step_entry construction, `_TranscriptWriter`, and M2 orchestrator design (#72).

**GRAB-3: Formal budget object** (`agentic-loop.md` lines 116-141)
The repo codifies `max_model_turns`, `max_tool_calls`, `max_parallel_tool_calls`, `max_wall_time_seconds`, `max_input_tokens`, `max_output_tokens`, `max_total_cost`, `max_tool_result_chars`, `max_retries_per_model_call`, `max_retries_per_tool_call`. datum has `max_steps`, `timeout_s`, and `MAX_*` constants, but no unified budget object. A `Budget` dataclass would simplify M2 packet budgeting and the `write_todos` planning tool (#70) design.
**Lands in:** New `datum/budget.py` or addition to `schemas.py`; consumed by `agent_loop.py` and M2.

**GRAB-4: Compaction handoff format** (`context-memory-compaction.md` lines 196-239)
The repo's compaction handoff template (objective, user constraints, instructions loaded, active plan, key facts, actions taken, errors, pending tasks, next step, "do not redo") is more complete than datum's `_compact_history` which produces a one-line-per-step digest. The "do not redo" section alone would reduce post-compaction repeat work on a 30B model.
**Lands in:** `agent_loop.py` `_compact_history()`.

**GRAB-5: Retry safety classification** (`agentic-loop.md` lines 143-162)
The repo splits retries into safe-to-retry (transient API errors, idempotent reads, validation after model fixes) vs never-retry (payments, external sends, destructive actions, unclear idempotency). datum's loop retries everything the same way (the no-progress breaker fires generically). Classifying tool retries by safety category -- especially for `run_command` (which can be destructive) -- would prevent the loop from blindly retrying `rm` or `git push` if they ever enter the tool catalog.
**Lands in:** `agent_loop.py` retry/no-progress logic, tool metadata in `TOOL_CATALOG`.

**GRAB-6: Tool risk-class metadata on the catalog** (`tools-and-permissions.md` lines 76-92)
The repo assigns each tool a risk class: `read_only`, `compute_only`, `write_local`, `process_execution`, `destructive`. datum's `TOOL_CATALOG` has `(args_sig, description)` but no risk class. Adding a third tuple element (risk class enum) enables the retry classification above and feeds the M2 orchestrator's permission decisions for delegate_task (#72).
**Lands in:** `agent_loop.py` `TOOL_CATALOG` entries, new `ToolRiskClass` enum in `schemas.py`.

**GRAB-7: Entropy/garbage-collection workflow concept** (`agent-legibility-feedback-loops.md` lines 166-183)
The repo describes recurring cleanup: stale doc scanning, repeated-failure analysis, tool inventory cleanup, quality score updates. datum has `knowledge_drift.py`, `memory_audit.py`, and `rules_doctor.py` as individual pieces, but no scheduled GC pass that orchestrates them. A `datum gc` command that runs the three in sequence would prevent drift accumulation.
**Lands in:** New CLI subcommand `datum gc` wiring existing modules.

### 3. Notable ALREADY-DOs

| Practice (repo) | datum equivalent | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| "Model proposes, harness executes" (core stance) | THINK/DECIDE/ASSEMBLE/EXECUTE split | datum's architecture is a textbook case. The DECIDE model is pure extraction; Python assembles and executes. |
| Read-before-write enforcement | `read_paths`/`partial_read_paths` guards in `agent_loop.py` | datum enforces this mechanically with separate full-read vs partial-read tracking, including the overwrite-loss Defect-3 detector. The repo only suggests it in prose. |
| Syntax gate on writes | `_check_py_syntax()` pre-execution, `_lint_python()` post-write | datum does both AST parse rejection and content lint. The repo mentions "validate before execution" generically. |
| Loop detection + no-progress breaker | `recent_signatures` + `_no_progress_fired` | datum has two-tier loop breaking: signature repetition detector and the no-progress breaker that fires once with a corrective message before the hard detector kills the run. More sophisticated than the repo's generic "repeated failure threshold." |
| Prompt injection boundary | `prompt_sanitizer.py` (special tokens, invisible Unicode, rules hash-pinning) | datum strips model-special tokens, invisible Unicode, and pins rule hashes to detect tampering. The repo describes this in prose; datum does it in code. |
| Mechanical invariants over behavioral prompting | `_lint_python()` DPS-103/DPS-203/SEC-001 checks, `_detect_removed_defs()` | datum's lint rules fire as deterministic AST validators that return remediation messages to the model -- precisely the pattern the repo advocates. |
| Context compaction at a threshold | `context_window * checkpoint_pct` trigger, `_compact_history()` | Both systems use a percentage threshold. datum checkpoints before compacting. |
| Per-step transcript logging | `_TranscriptWriter` JSONL | datum writes append-only JSONL transcripts per episode. The repo recommends typed event traces. |
| "Start single-agent, add multi only when failures justify it" | datum is still single-agent; M2 multi-agent is on the roadmap only after measured gaps | Direct match with the repo's "do not design a multi-agent system before a single-agent loop has failed measurable evals." |

The convergence is strong. datum has structurally enforced most of the practices the repo describes behaviorally.

### 4. SKIPs worth flagging

**SKIP: Prompt caching / cache-aware ordering** (`prompt-caching-and-cost.md`)
The entire document is about API-provider prefix caching (OpenAI automatic, Anthropic cache breakpoints). datum runs on a local oMLX server. oMLX has its own KV cache semantics (controlled by `kv_bits`, `max_kv_size` in config). The stable-prefix advice is already baked into `_build_system_prompt()` being static per run. The provider-specific cache monitoring (logged `cached_tokens` fields, TTL tuning, cache hit rate per tenant) is irrelevant and would add dead telemetry.

**SKIP: MCP / external connectors / skill progressive disclosure** (`skills-and-connectors.md`)
datum-local is a self-contained local pipeline. It has no MCP servers, no external connectors, no skill registry. The progressive-disclosure pattern is a good idea for hosted agents but adds architecture for no benefit here. If M2 ever grows external tool sources, revisit.

**SKIP: Approval manager / draft-commit split** (`tools-and-permissions.md` lines 148-163)
datum's TDD loop is fully autonomous within a sandboxed branch. There is no human-in-the-loop approval gate because the pipeline operates on throwaway feature branches that require human PR review as the external gate. Adding an approval manager inside the loop would halt the autonomous RED-GREEN-REFACTOR cycle for no safety gain -- the blast radius is already bounded by branch isolation.

**SKIP: Permission engine with deny/ask_user/approval_required decisions** (`tools-and-permissions.md` lines 119-145)
Same reasoning as above. datum's "permission" is the allowed_tools list -- binary allow/block. A decision engine returning `approval_required` would block the loop on a model that has no user to ask. The read-before-write and syntax gates are the actual permission enforcement, and they are deterministic, not LLM-decided.

**SKIP: Goal-like loop / long-running objectives** (`planning-and-goals.md` lines 155-257)
A 30B local model cannot maintain coherent goal state across sessions. datum's loop is episodic by design: each TDD phase is one bounded episode. The M2 orchestrator (#70-72) will manage cross-phase continuity from Python, not from the model's perspective. Loading goal-loop state into the 30B model's prompt would consume context and produce hallucinated progress claims.

**SKIP: Autonomy levels / maturity model** (`architecture.md` lines 143-169)
A useful taxonomy for greenfield design, but datum has already made its choice: it is a Level 3 policy-bounded actor (writes code, runs tests) within a structurally sandboxed branch. Retro-fitting a maturity model to a working system adds governance overhead with no design benefit.

### 5. Clone path

`/Volumes/Extra/repos/gitrdunhq/datum/.temp/agents-best-practices-crib`

---

**Bottom line:** The repo is a solid field guide that validates most of datum's design choices -- particularly the harness-executes-model-proposes split, mechanical invariants, read-before-write, syntax gating, and loop detection. The seven GRABs above are concrete improvements: structured tool results with next-valid-actions, typed event log, unified budget object, richer compaction handoff, retry safety classification, tool risk-class metadata, and a GC command. All are small, incremental additions that land in existing files. The practices aimed at hosted API agents (caching telemetry, MCP, approval flows, goal loops) do not fit a 30B local-model pipeline and should be skipped.</result>

---

Here is the full assessment.

## 1. What the repo/doc is

**celesteanders/harness** is a lightweight Claude Code orchestration kit: one best-practices doc (consolidation of Anthropic + OpenAI + Huntley research), a headless runner (`runner.py`), a separate evaluator agent (`evaluator.py`), a Claude Code skill (`/harness`), and the four source research articles. 151 stars, 24 forks, single commit (Apr 2, 2026), Python, no license declared. It targets Claude Code sessions (via `claude -p`) as the execution engine. Not a framework -- more of a recipe + reference harness.

## 2. GRAB list (ranked by value)

### GRAB-1: Separate Evaluator Agent in a Fresh Context (Section 1 + evaluator.py)

**What:** A skeptical evaluator that runs in a *separate* context window with read-only permissions, grading each task against acceptance criteria with concrete PASS/FAIL verdicts. The key insight: "tuning a standalone evaluator to be skeptical is far more tractable than making a generator critical of its own work." Their evaluator checks: verification passes, ACs satisfied, test quality, no placeholders, TDD compliance -- with few-shot FAIL examples for calibration.

**Where it lands in datum:** This fills the gap between `agent_loop.py` (which builds + executes) and the gate system (`gate.py`, which validates artifacts structurally). Right now, the agent loop has no post-implementation quality review step -- the loop ends when the tool chain converges. A `verify_phase.py` evaluator subagent (or an evaluator mode in the existing agent_loop) could run after act_green/act_refactor succeeds, using a separate `generate()` call with a skeptical system prompt. It would read the diff, run pytest, check for placeholders, and produce a structured verdict. The verdict feeds back as an OBSERVE step. Fits the THINK/DECIDE split: the evaluator only needs the think model (read + analyze), no DECIDE required since it writes no code. Max 2 retry cycles before escalation -- matches datum's existing corrective-retry pattern in the TDD phases.

**Value:** High. This is the single biggest architectural gap between datum and the harness pattern. datum trusts pytest convergence as the quality signal, but "tests pass" is not the same as "tests are meaningful." The placeholder-grep and AC-checklist checks are cheap and mechanical.

### GRAB-2: Sprint Contract / Pre-Implementation Agreement (Section 4)

**What:** Before each implementation chunk, the generator and evaluator negotiate what "done" looks like. The generator proposes what it will build and how success is verified; the evaluator reviews the proposal before work begins.

**Where it lands:** Currently, TOML task specs define acceptance criteria up front, but there is no verification that the agent *understood* the spec before coding starts. A lightweight "contract confirmation" step at the top of act_red -- the THINK model restates the ACs in its own words, and the DECIDE model extracts a structured checklist -- would catch misunderstandings before code is written. This is essentially a "plan" sub-step within each act phase. Cheap to implement: one extra THINK+DECIDE pair before the first tool call.

**Value:** Medium-high. Misinterpretation of specs is a real failure mode at 30B scale.

### GRAB-3: Structured Session Handoff Artifacts (Sections 2 + 3)

**What:** JSON-based task state (not markdown), progress notes, git history read at session start. The harness prescribes: "JSON resists model-induced corruption better than freeform Markdown. Rules: never remove or reorder items, only flip status from incomplete to complete."

**Where it lands:** datum already uses `.datum/state.json` and TOML specs, so the JSON-over-markdown principle is established. But the *session lifecycle protocol* -- orient (read state + git log), verify baseline, select one task, implement, update state, clean exit -- is worth codifying into the M2 orchestrator. Specifically: the "verify baseline before building" step. Right now, the TDD driver jumps straight into RED without confirming the repo is green. A `pytest -q` baseline check at the top of each act phase (before the agent loop starts) would catch regressions from prior phases.

**Value:** Medium. The baseline-verify step is the concrete grab here; the rest is reinforcement of existing patterns.

### GRAB-4: Evaluator Few-Shot FAIL Calibration (evaluator.py lines 187-208)

**What:** The evaluator prompt includes concrete examples of common FAIL patterns: "test exists but doesn't verify the AC" (mocks too aggressively), "function exists but isn't wired in" (dead code), "placeholder hiding behind interface." These few-shot examples calibrate the model's skepticism threshold.

**Where it lands:** If/when we build the evaluator from GRAB-1, these exact examples should go into the evaluator's system prompt. For a 30B model, few-shot calibration is even more important than for frontier models -- the model needs concrete anchors for what "FAIL" looks like. These examples can live in `datum/assets/evaluator_examples.toml` alongside the existing prompt templates.

**Value:** Medium. Only useful in conjunction with GRAB-1, but basically free to adopt.

### GRAB-5: Context Offload to Subagents for Read-Only Ops (Section 5)

**What:** "The primary context window should operate as a scheduler. Spawn subagents for file system searches, code analysis, test execution, and summarization." Fan out read-only work with high parallelism, limit write parallelism.

**Where it lands:** Directly relevant to `delegate_task` (#72) on the roadmap. The harness's framing -- primary loop as scheduler, subagents as workers -- is the right mental model for M2. datum's agent_loop already does tool dispatch inline; the M2 orchestrator should push analysis-heavy phases (triage, validate, landscape) into subagent calls while keeping the act phases (which write code) sequential in the primary loop. This is architecture guidance, not a code grab.

**Value:** Medium. Confirms the M2 direction rather than adding new information.

### GRAB-6: Command Allowlist Parsing (Section 7)

**What:** "Parse commands with `shlex`, handle pipes/chaining, and block anything not explicitly allowed. Add validation for sensitive commands."

**Where it lands:** datum's `_execute_tool` in agent_loop.py runs `run_command` through a sandbox, but the security plan (S0-S3) is still proposed. The shlex-parse + allowlist approach is a concrete implementation pattern for S0. Worth referencing in the security spec: parse the command string with `shlex.split()`, check each token against an allowlist, reject pipes/semicolons/backticks that could chain unauthorized commands.

**Value:** Medium-low. We know we need this; the harness confirms the approach but doesn't add novel technique.

### GRAB-7: Plan Regeneration from Codebase vs Spec (Section 9)

**What:** "Periodically delete and regenerate [todo lists] by having the agent compare the current codebase against the specification. This prevents the agent from following stale or incorrect plans."

**Where it lands:** datum has `spec_drift_detector.py` which partially addresses this. But the harness frames it as a *proactive* step: regenerate the plan, don't just detect drift. For the M2 orchestrator, after each act phase completes, a lightweight "plan still valid?" check -- comparing remaining tasks against actual code state -- could prevent wasted work. This is a refinement to spec_drift_detector: instead of just flagging drift, it should propose plan mutations.

**Value:** Low-medium. spec_drift_detector already exists; this is an incremental improvement.

## 3. Notable ALREADY-DOs (convergences)

- **TDD as the implementation loop**: harness prescribes "write failing tests first, then implement." datum's act_red/act_green/act_refactor phases are this exact pattern, more rigorously enforced (AST baseline preservation, pytest-output classification, corrective retry per phase).

- **No-placeholder enforcement**: harness greps for TODO/FIXME/pass/NotImplementedError. datum's agent_loop has the `ast.parse` syntax gate and the overwrite-loss detector. The placeholder grep would be additive (GRAB-1), but the structural enforcement is already stronger.

- **Git as safety net with descriptive commits**: harness prescribes commit-after-task. datum has `commit_queue.py` for this.

- **One task per session / context conservation**: harness says "one task per session." datum's pipeline_scheduler and lane system enforce this.

- **Prevent placeholder/stub implementations**: harness uses strong language in prompts. datum enforces this structurally (ast.parse gate rejects broken code, read-before-write prevents silent overwrites).

- **Capture bugs immediately**: harness says "document bugs in the plan." datum has `report_bug.py` and the `datum bugfile` CLI.

- **Repo as single source of truth**: harness stores everything in-repo. datum uses `.datum/` for all state.

- **Diagnose before retry**: harness has MAX_RETRIES=2 then exit. datum has `diagnose_failure.py` with ENVIRONMENTAL/REASONING/UNKNOWN classification -- actually more sophisticated.

## 4. SKIPs worth flagging

- **Browser/UI automation with Playwright MCP (Section 4)**: The harness prescribes Puppeteer/Playwright for verification. datum is a CLI tool pipeline, not a web app. No UI to test. SKIP.

- **Context resets via fresh Claude sessions (Section 1)**: The harness spawns new `claude -p` sessions for context isolation. datum runs local 30B models where context windows are ~8K-32K tokens (vs 200K for Claude). We already *must* work in tight context; fresh sessions are our only option by default. The "compaction vs reset" discussion is a frontier-model luxury. SKIP (already forced by hardware).

- **Allow agent to self-improve CLAUDE.md (Section 6)**: Dangerous at 30B scale. A local model editing its own instruction set is a recipe for instruction drift and hallucinated "improvements." datum's rules-hash-pinning in `prompt_sanitizer.py` exists specifically to *prevent* this. SKIP (actively harmful in our architecture).

- **Git tags for known-good states + git reset --hard recovery (Section 9)**: datum's `rollback.py` already handles this, and `test_ratchet.py` prevents regression. The harness's approach is more manual (human decides when to reset). SKIP (already automated).

- **JSON plans over Markdown (Section 2)**: The harness argues JSON resists corruption. datum uses TOML for specs (human-editable, structurally validated) and JSON for machine state. The TOML choice is deliberate -- it's easier for humans to write ACs in TOML than JSON. SKIP (intentional divergence).

- **"Boring" technology stack for agent legibility (Section 8)**: Good advice for teams using frontier models on web stacks. datum already runs minimal deps, pure Python, no frameworks. SKIP (already aligned, just phrased differently).

- **Per-git-worktree app instances for isolated testing (Section 8)**: Relevant for web apps with running servers. datum tests via pytest, not live instances. SKIP.
